I just know that I've always struggled with it, but I haven't paid close attention to how it affects me differently at home versus work.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, and is that worry hard to control even when you try to relax?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I experience excessive or uncontrollable worrying about a lot of different things. My main frustration is just with my disorganization, but I don't really feel constantly anxious or worried in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy for at least two weeks, especially around the times your concentration feels worse?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling down or losing interest in the things I enjoy. My struggles are definitely more about my focus and organization rather than any kind of mood issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes, lose things, get easily distracted, or avoid tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about making careless mistakes or losing things, but I definitely tend to avoid or put off tasks that require a lot of sustained mental effort. I feel a strong reluctance to even start projects that need long, focused concentration. That struggle is very real for me.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me an example of how these problems show up both at home and at work or school? For instance, do you miss deadlines, forget appointments, or have trouble finishing chores in both places?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely happens in both settings. At work, I really struggle to organize my tasks and structure my projects, which makes managing my workload very difficult. At home, I often forget appointments and have a really tough time actually finishing chores.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often have trouble sustaining attention during conversations, reading, or meetings, or find that you don’t seem to listen even when someone is speaking directly to you?\n\nPatient: I don't really experience that issue, to be honest. I haven't noticed having trouble listening when people talk to me, or paying attention during conversations and reading."

My struggles are definitely more about my focus and organization rather than any kind of mood issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes, lose things, get easily distracted, or avoid tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about making careless mistakes or losing things, but I definitely tend to avoid or put off tasks that require a lot of sustained mental effort. I feel a strong reluctance to even start projects that need long, focused concentration. That struggle is very real for me.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me an example of how these problems show up both at home and at work or school? For instance, do you miss deadlines, forget appointments, or have trouble finishing chores in both places?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely happens in both settings. At work, I really struggle to organize my tasks and structure my projects, which makes managing my workload very difficult. At home, I often forget appointments and have a really tough time actually finishing chores.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often have trouble sustaining attention during conversations, reading, or meetings, or find that you don’t seem to listen even when someone is speaking directly to you?\n\nPatient: I don't really experience that issue, to be honest. I haven't noticed having trouble listening when people talk to me, or paying attention during conversations and reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you often get distracted by unrelated thoughts or things going on around you, or find that you start tasks but leave them unfinished because your attention shifts?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't really get easily distracted by outside noises or thoughts, and I don't typically leave tasks unfinished once I actually get them started. My main difficulties are really with organizing tasks to begin with, avoiding things that take a lot of mental effort, or simply forgetting daily appointments and chores.

Was there any major stress or mood change around the time they began?\n\nPatient: Yes, these struggles with organization have actually been going on since I was a kid, definitely before I turned 12. I don't recall any major stress or mood changes triggering them, as it has just been a long-standing issue for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and organization problems happen in more than one setting, like both at work or school and at home, and have they caused problems with your performance or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure, to be honest, as I haven't really thought about it in that way before. I just know that I've always struggled with it, but I haven't paid close attention to how it affects me differently at home versus work.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about many different things, and is that worry hard to control even when you try to relax?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I experience excessive or uncontrollable worrying about a lot of different things. My main frustration is just with my disorganization, but I don't really feel constantly anxious or worried in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy for at least two weeks, especially around the times your concentration feels worse?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling down or losing interest in the things I enjoy. My struggles are definitely more about my focus and organization rather than any kind of mood issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes, lose things, get easily distracted, or avoid tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about making careless mistakes or losing things, but I definitely tend to avoid or put off tasks that require a lot of sustained mental effort. I feel a strong reluctance to even start projects that need long, focused concentration. That struggle is very real for me.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me an example of how these problems show up both at home and at work or school? For instance, do you miss deadlines, forget appointments, or have trouble finishing chores in both places?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely happens in both settings. At work, I really struggle to organize my tasks and structure my projects, which makes managing my workload very difficult. At home, I often forget appointments and have a really tough time actually finishing chores.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often have trouble sustaining attention during conversations, reading, or meetings, or find that you don’t seem to listen even when someone is speaking directly to you?\n\nPatient: I don't really experience that issue, to be honest. I haven't noticed having trouble listening when people talk to me, or paying attention during conversations and reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you often get distracted by unrelated thoughts or things going on around you, or find that you start tasks but leave them unfinished because your attention shifts?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get easily distracted by outside noises or thoughts, and I don't typically leave tasks unfinished once I actually get them started. My main difficulties are really with organizing tasks to begin with, avoiding things that take a lot of mental effort, or simply forgetting daily appointments and chores.\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get easily distracted by outside noises or thoughts, and I don't typically leave tasks unfinished once I actually get them started. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
